 \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda s^\alpha}s^{n+\beta-1}\dd s
 =\frac1\alpha\Gamma\!\left(\frac{n+\beta}{\alpha}\right)
 \lambda^{-(n+\beta)/\alpha},
\]
and Watson's lemma controls the replacement of the finite endpoint interval by
the asymptotic expansion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The appearance of ordinary rather than exponential Bell polynomials is forced by
the ordinary Taylor expansion of $A(t)^{-\rho}$.  Lagrange inversion enters in the
change from $t$ to the natural Laplace coordinate $s$; the gamma factor enters only
in the final monomial integral.
\end{remark}

\part{Incidence, Umbral, and Matrix Methods}
\label{part:incidence-umbral}

\chapter{Inversion on finite sets and partition lattices}
\label{ch:incidence-inversion}

The five drafts use several inversions that look different only because they are
written in different coordinates.  Binomial inversion is Möbius inversion on the
Boolean lattice, Stirling inversion is change of basis between powers and factorial
powers, and moment--cumulant inversion is Möbius inversion on the partition lattice.
This chapter puts these statements in one framework and supplies the details that
were abbreviated in the drafts.

\section{Binomial inversion}

\begin{theorem}[Binomial inversion]\label{thm:new-binomial-inversion}
For sequences $(a_n)_{n\ge0}$ and $(b_n)_{n\ge0}$ over a commutative ring,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-binomial-forward}
 b_n=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk a_k
\end{equation}
for every $n$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-binomial-inverse}
 a_n=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^{n-k}\binom nk b_k.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, if $A(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nz^n/n!$ and
$B(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}b_nz^n/n!$, then $B(z)=e^zA(z)$ and
$A(z)=e^{-z}B(z)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substitute \eqref{eq:new-binomial-forward} into the right side of
\eqref{eq:new-binomial-inverse} and interchange the two finite sums:
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^{n-k}\binom nk b_k
 &=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^{n-k}\binom nk
   \sum_{j=0}^k\binom kj a_j\\
 &=\sum_{j=0}^na_j\binom nj
   \sum_{k=j}^n(-1)^{n-k}\binom{n-j}{k-j}.
\end{align*}
The inner sum is $(1-1)^{n-j}$, hence it is $1$ when $j=n$ and $0$
otherwise.  The result is $a_n$.  The reverse implication follows by the same
calculation with the roles of the two formulas interchanged.  For the generating
functions, multiplication by $e^z$ gives
\[
 n!\coeff z n e^zA(z)=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk a_k=b_n,
\]
and multiplication by $e^{-z}$ gives the inverse relation.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The sign $(-1)^{n-k}$ is the Möbius function of the Boolean interval from a
$k$-element subset to an $n$-element set.  Thus the theorem is the simplest
instance of incidence-algebra inversion.
\end{remark}

\section{The Möbius function of the partition lattice}

Let $\Pi_n$ be the lattice of set partitions of $[n]$, ordered by refinement.
For $\sigma\le\pi$, and for each block $B$ of $\pi$, let $m_B$ be the number
of blocks of $\sigma$ contained in $B$.

\begin{theorem}[Partition-lattice Möbius function]\label{thm:new-partition-mobius}
The Möbius function of $\Pi_n$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-partition-mobius}
 \mu(\sigma,\pi)=
 (-1)^{|\sigma|-|\pi|}\prod_{B\in\pi}(m_B-1)!.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-partition-mobius-top}
 \mu(\pi,\widehat 1)=(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The interval $[\sigma,\pi]$ is the direct product of the lattices
$\Pi_{m_B}$, one for each block $B$ of $\pi$: passing from $\sigma$ to a
coarser partition inside $B$ means partitioning the $m_B$ blocks of $\sigma$
that lie in $B$.  The Möbius function is multiplicative on direct products, so
it remains to compute
\[
 u_m:=\mu_{\Pi_m}(\widehat0,\widehat1).
\]
For a partition $\tau\in\Pi_m$, the interval $[\widehat0,\tau]$ is the
product of $\Pi_{|C|}$ over the blocks $C$ of $\tau$.  Hence
\[
 \mu(\widehat0,\tau)=\prod_{C\in\tau}u_{|C|}.
\]
The defining Möbius relation says
\[
 \sum_{\tau\in\Pi_m}\prod_{C\in\tau}u_{|C|}
 =\begin{cases}1,&m=0,1,\\0,&m\ge2,
 \end{cases}
\]
where the empty partition contributes $1$ for $m=0$.  By the labelled
exponential formula, the exponential generating function of the left side is
\[
 \exp\left(\sum_{m\ge1}u_m\frac{z^m}{m!}\right).
\]
The displayed values therefore force this exponential to equal $1+z$.
Taking the formal logarithm gives
\[
 \sum_{m\ge1}u_m\frac{z^m}{m!}=\log(1+z)
 =\sum_{m\ge1}(-1)^{m-1}\frac{z^m}{m},
\]
so $u_m=(-1)^{m-1}(m-1)!$.  Multiplication over the blocks of $\pi$ yields
\eqref{eq:new-partition-mobius}; taking $\pi=\widehat1$ yields
\eqref{eq:new-partition-mobius-top}.
\end{proof}

\section{Moments and cumulants as Möbius transforms}

Let $(m_n)_{n\ge0}$ and $(\kappa_n)_{n\ge1}$ be related by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-moment-partition}
 m_n=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_n}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|},
 \qquad m_0=1.
\end{equation}
This is the set-partition form of the moment--cumulant relation.

\begin{corollary}[Cumulant inversion]\label{cor:new-cumulant-inversion}
The inverse relation is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-cumulant-partition}
 \kappa_n=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_n}
 (-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!
 \prod_{B\in\pi}m_{|B|}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, if
\[
 M(z)=1+\sum_{n\ge1}m_n\frac{z^n}{n!},\qquad
 K(z)=\sum_{n\ge1}\kappa_n\frac{z^n}{n!},
\]
then $M(z)=\exp K(z)$ and $K(z)=\log M(z)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{eq:new-moment-partition} says that the multiplicative function
built from the $m_n$ is the zeta transform of the multiplicative function built
from the $\kappa_n$ on the partition lattice.  Möbius inversion and
\eqref{eq:new-partition-mobius-top} give
\eqref{eq:new-cumulant-partition}.  Grouping partitions by their block-size
multiplicities gives the complete Bell-polynomial form, and the exponential
formula gives $M=\exp K$.  Taking the formal logarithm gives the inverse.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Binomial type, Sheffer sequences, and exponential Riordan arrays}
\label{ch:sheffer-riordan}

\section{Polynomial sequences of binomial type}

\begin{definition}
A sequence $(p_n(x))_{n\ge0}$ with $p_0(x)=1$ and $\deg p_n=n$ is of
\emph{binomial type} if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-binomial-type}
 p_n(x+y)=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk p_k(x)p_{n-k}(y).
\end{equation}
A formal series $\beta(z)=\beta_1z+\beta_2z^2+\cdots$ with
$\beta_1\ne0$ is called a delta series.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Delta-series characterization]\label{thm:new-binomial-type}
Over a characteristic-zero field, $(p_n)$ is of binomial type if and only if
there is a unique delta series $\beta$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-binomial-type-egf}
 \sum_{n\ge0}p_n(x)\frac{z^n}{n!}=\exp\bigl(x\beta(z)\bigr).
\end{equation}
For this series,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-binomial-type-bell}
 p_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n x^k
 \BellP{n}{k}(\beta_1,\beta_2,\ldots,\beta_{n-k+1}).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If \eqref{eq:new-binomial-type-egf} holds, then the product of the generating
functions at $x$ and $y$ is the generating function at $x+y$; coefficient
comparison gives \eqref{eq:new-binomial-type}.

Conversely, put $P(x,z)=\sum p_n(x)z^n/n!$.  The binomial identity is exactly
$P(x+y,z)=P(x,z)P(y,z)$, while $P(0,z)=1$.  Hence the formal logarithm
$L(x,z)=\log P(x,z)$ satisfies $L(x+y,z)=L(x,z)+L(y,z)$.  For each fixed
power of $z$, its coefficient is a polynomial in $x$ that is additive.
In characteristic zero every additive polynomial is linear, so
$L(x,z)=x\beta(z)$.  The degree condition forces $\beta(0)=0$ and
$\beta_1\ne0$, and uniqueness is clear from $\beta(z)=L(1,z)$.
Finally, expand
\[
 e^{x\beta(z)}=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{x^k}{k!}\beta(z)^k
\]
and use the defining exponential generating function of the partial Bell
polynomials.  This gives \eqref{eq:new-binomial-type-bell}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
The choices $\beta(z)=z$, $\log(1+z)$, $-\log(1-z)$, and $e^z-1$ give,
respectively,
\[
 x^n,\qquad \falling{x}{n},\qquad \rising{x}{n},\qquad
 T_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n\stirtwo nk x^k.
\]
Thus monomials, factorial powers, and Touchard polynomials are coordinates in
one delta-series calculus.
\end{example}

\section{Sheffer sequences and lowering operators}

\begin{definition}
A polynomial sequence $(s_n(x))_{n\ge0}$ is a \emph{Sheffer sequence} for
$(A,\beta)$ if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-sheffer-egf}
 \sum_{n\ge0}s_n(x)\frac{z^n}{n!}
 =A(z)e^{x\beta(z)},
 \qquad A(0)\ne0,
\end{equation}
where $\beta$ is a delta series.
\end{definition}

Let $(p_n)$ be the binomial-type sequence associated with $\beta$ and write
$A(z)=\sum a_jz^j/j!$.

\begin{proposition}[Sheffer addition and convolution]\label{prop:new-sheffer}
One has
\begin{align}
 s_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk s_k(x)p_{n-k}(y),
 \label{eq:new-sheffer-addition}\\
 s_n(x)&=\sum_{j=0}^n\binom nj a_jp_{n-j}(x).
 \label{eq:new-sheffer-convolution}
\end{align}
If $\bar\beta$ is the compositional inverse of $\beta$ and
$Q=\bar\beta(D_x)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-delta-lowering}
 Qp_n(x)=np_{n-1}(x).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Multiplying the EGF at $x$ by $e^{y\beta(z)}$ gives the EGF at $x+y$ and
proves \eqref{eq:new-sheffer-addition}.  Multiplication of the EGFs of $A$
and $p_n$ gives \eqref{eq:new-sheffer-convolution}.  Finally,
$D_xe^{x\beta(z)}=\beta(z)e^{x\beta(z)}$.  Formal functional calculus therefore
gives
\[
 \bar\beta(D_x)e^{x\beta(z)}
 =\bar\beta(\beta(z))e^{x\beta(z)}
 =ze^{x\beta(z)}.
\]
Comparison of coefficients proves \eqref{eq:new-delta-lowering}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Appell sequences are the special case $\beta(z)=z$.  The Bernoulli and Euler
polynomials developed later are Appell sequences with prefactors
$t/(e^t-1)$ and $2/(e^t+1)$, respectively.  Lagrange inversion enters the
operational theory through the coefficients of $\bar\beta$.
\end{remark}

\section{Exponential Riordan arrays}

For $g(0)\ne0$ and $f(z)=f_1z+O(z^2)$ with $f_1\ne0$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-riordan-entry}
 R_{n,k}=\frac{n!}{k!}\coeff z n g(z)f(z)^k.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Riordan action, product, and inverse]\label{thm:new-riordan}
If $(a_k)$ has EGF $A(z)$, the transformed sequence
$b_n=\sum_{k=0}^nR_{n,k}a_k$ has EGF
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-riordan-action}
 B(z)=g(z)A(f(z)).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{align}
 (g,f)(h,\ell)&=\bigl(g\,h\!\circ f,\,\ell\!\circ f\bigr),
 \label{eq:new-riordan-product}\\
 (g,f)^{-1}&=\left(\frac1{g\circ\bar f},\bar f\right),
 \label{eq:new-riordan-inverse}
\end{align}
where $\bar f=f^{\langle-1\rangle}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Using \eqref{eq:new-riordan-entry},
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{n\ge0}b_n\frac{z^n}{n!}
 &=\sum_{k\ge0}a_k\sum_{n\ge k}\frac{R_{n,k}}{n!}z^n\\
 &=g(z)\sum_{k\ge0}a_k\frac{f(z)^k}{k!}=g(z)A(f(z)).
\end{align*}
Applying first $(h,\ell)$ and then $(g,f)$ sends $A$ to
$g(z)h(f(z))A(\ell(f(z)))$, proving the product law.  Substituting
$\bar f$ into the product and choosing the prefactor $1/(g\circ\bar f)$
gives the identity array $(1,z)$, proving the inverse formula.
\end{proof}

The principal coefficient arrays of this monograph are Riordan arrays:
\begin{align}
 \stirtwo nk&=\frac{n!}{k!}\coeff z n(e^z-1)^k,
 \label{eq:new-s2-riordan}\\
 s(n,k)&=\frac{n!}{k!}\coeff z n\log(1+z)^k,
 \label{eq:new-s1-riordan}\\
 \BellP nk(x_1,x_2,\ldots)
 &=\frac{n!}{k!}\coeff z n
 \left(\sum_{j\ge1}x_j\frac{z^j}{j!}\right)^k.
 \label{eq:new-bell-riordan}
\end{align}
Since $e^z-1$ and $\log(1+z)$ are compositional inverses, Stirling matrix
inversion is the special case of \eqref{eq:new-riordan-inverse} with $g=1$.
Faà di Bruno composition is matrix multiplication in the same group.

\section{Abel polynomials from Lagrange inversion}

\begin{theorem}[Abel binomial sequence]\label{thm:new-abel}
For a fixed scalar $a$, the polynomials
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-abel-polynomials}
 A_n(x)=x(x-an)^{n-1}\quad(n\ge1),\qquad A_0(x)=1,
\end{equation}
form a sequence of binomial type.  Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-abel-identity}
 (x+y)(x+y-an)^{n-1}
 =\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk
 x(x-ak)^{k-1}y\bigl(y-a(n-k)\bigr)^{n-k-1},
\end{equation}
with the endpoint factors interpreted by continuity as $A_0=1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $T(z)$ be the unique formal solution of
$T=ze^{-aT}$.  Lagrange--Bürmann inversion gives, for $n\ge1$,
\begin{align*}
 \coeff z n e^{xT(z)}
 &=\frac{x}{n}\coeff u{n-1}e^{xu}e^{-anu}\\
 &=\frac{x(x-an)^{n-1}}{n!}.
\end{align*}
Therefore
\[
 e^{xT(z)}=\sum_{n\ge0}A_n(x)\frac{z^n}{n!}.
\]
This is of the form \eqref{eq:new-binomial-type-egf}; the delta-series
characterization proves binomial type, and coefficient comparison in
$e^{(x+y)T}=e^{xT}e^{yT}$ gives \eqref{eq:new-abel-identity}.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Triangular algorithms and independent consistency checks}
\label{ch:series-algorithms}

\section{Elementary operations on ordinary series}

Let $A(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nz^n$, $B(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}b_nz^n$, and
$C(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}c_nz^n$.  The following formulas are triangular: once
coefficients of order below $n$ are known, they determine the coefficient of
order $n$.

\begin{proposition}[Product, reciprocal, and quotient]\label{prop:new-basic-algorithms}
For $C=AB$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-product-recursion}
 c_n=\sum_{j=0}^na_jb_{n-j}.
\end{equation}
If $a_0\ne0$ and $C=1/A$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-reciprocal-recursion}
 c_0=a_0^{-1},\qquad
 c_n=-a_0^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^na_jc_{n-j}.
\end{equation}
If $C=B/A$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-quotient-recursion}
 c_n=a_0^{-1}\left(b_n-\sum_{j=1}^na_jc_{n-j}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
All three statements are obtained by equating the coefficient of $z^n$ in
$C=AB$, $AC=1$, and $AC=B$, respectively, and isolating the term containing
$c_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Logarithm, exponential, and arbitrary powers]\label{prop:new-log-exp-power}
Assume $a_0=1$.  If $L=\log A=\sum_{n\ge1}\ell_nz^n$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-log-recursion}
 \ell_n=a_n-\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}j\ell_j a_{n-j}.
\end{equation}
Conversely, if $A=e^L$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-exp-recursion}
 a_0=1,\qquad a_n=\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n j\ell_j a_{n-j}.
\end{equation}
For $C=A^\alpha$ with $c_0=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-power-recursion}
 c_n=\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n\bigl((\alpha+1)j-n\bigr)a_jc_{n-j}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The identity $A'=AL'$ gives
$n a_n=\sum_{j=1}^n j\ell_j a_{n-j}$; solving either for $\ell_n$ or for
$a_n$ proves the first two recurrences.  For the power, logarithmic
differentiation gives $AC'=\alpha A'C$.  The coefficient of $z^{n-1}$ is
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}(n-j)a_jc_{n-j}
 =\alpha\sum_{j=1}^n j a_jc_{n-j}.
\]
Isolating $nc_n$ gives \eqref{eq:new-power-recursion}; its $j=n$ term is
$\alpha a_n$.
\end{proof}

\section{Composition and reversion}

If $B(0)=0$, the ordinary Bell polynomials give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-ordinary-composition-algorithm}
 \coeff z n A(B(z))
 =\sum_{k=0}^n a_k\,\coeff z n B(z)^k.
\end{equation}
The coefficients of each power $B^k$ follow from repeated Cauchy products, or
from the ordinary partial Bell-polynomial recurrence.  If
$F(z)=a_1z+a_2z^2+\cdots$ with $a_1\ne0$, its inverse $G$ is determined by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-reversion-algorithm}
 \coeff z n G(z)^m
 =\frac{m}{n}\coeff u{n-m}\left(\frac{u}{F(u)}\right)^n,
 \qquad 1\le m\le n,
\end{equation}
which is the power form of Lagrange inversion.  Thus product, quotient,
powers, logarithm, exponential, composition, and reversion all reduce to
finite convolution and coefficient extraction.

\section{Low-order inverse certificates}

\begin{proposition}[Direct reversion through degree five]\label{prop:new-low-order-reversion}
For
\[
 F(z)=z+a_2z^2+a_3z^3+a_4z^4+a_5z^5+O(z^6),
\]
the compositional inverse is
\begin{align*}
 F^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)
={}&z-a_2z^2+(2a_2^2-a_3)z^3\\
 &+(-5a_2^3+5a_2a_3-a_4)z^4\\
 &+(14a_2^4-21a_2^2a_3+3a_3^2+6a_2a_4-a_5)z^5
 +O(z^6).
\end{align*}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $G(z)=z+b_2z^2+b_3z^3+b_4z^4+b_5z^5+O(z^6)$ and expand
$F(G(z))$.  The coefficients of $z^2,z^3,z^4,z^5$ are respectively
\begin{align*}
 &b_2+a_2,\\
 &b_3+2a_2b_2+a_3,\\
 &b_4+a_2(2b_3+b_2^2)+3a_3b_2+a_4,\\
 &b_5+a_2(2b_4+2b_2b_3)
   +a_3(3b_3+3b_2^2)+4a_4b_2+a_5.
\end{align*}
Setting them equal to zero and solving successively gives the displayed
coefficients.  This finite calculation independently certifies the Bell- and
Lagrange-based inverse formulas through fourth nontrivial order.
\end{proof}

\part{Bernoulli, Euler, Genocchi, and Summation Calculus}
\label{part:classical-polynomials}

\chapter{Bernoulli polynomials and their two kinds}
\label{ch:bernoulli-polynomials}

\section{Definition and Appell structure}

The Bernoulli polynomials $\beta_n(x)$ are defined by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-bernoulli-egf}
 \frac{t e^{xt}}{e^t-1}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}\beta_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}.
\end{equation}
Thus $\beta_n=\beta_n(0)$, with $\beta_1=-1/2$.  The identity is valid
formally in $t$ and analytically for $|t|<2\pi$.  These normalizations and the
parallel Euler-polynomial conventions agree with the authoritative tables in
\cite{NISTDLMF}; the finite-difference viewpoint is classical in
\cite{Norlund1924}.

\begin{theorem}[Basic Bernoulli-polynomial calculus]\label{thm:new-bernoulli-basic}
For $n\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \beta_n'(x)&=n\beta_{n-1}(x), &&n\ge1,
 \label{eq:new-bernoulli-derivative}\\
 \beta_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk\beta_k(x)y^{n-k},
 \label{eq:new-bernoulli-addition}\\
 \beta_n(x+1)-\beta_n(x)&=nx^{n-1}, &&n\ge1,
 \label{eq:new-bernoulli-difference}\\
 \beta_n(1-x)&=(-1)^n\beta_n(x),
 \label{eq:new-bernoulli-reflection}\\
 \int_0^1\beta_n(x)\dd x&=0, &&n\ge1.
 \label{eq:new-bernoulli-mean-zero}
\end{align}
For every positive integer $q$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-bernoulli-multiplication}
 \beta_n(qx)=q^{n-1}\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}
 \beta_n\left(x+\frac rq\right).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate \eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-egf} with respect to $x$ and compare
coefficients to obtain \eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-derivative}.  Multiplication of
the EGF by $e^{yt}$ gives \eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-addition}.  For the finite
difference,
\[
 \frac{t e^{(x+1)t}}{e^t-1}-\frac{t e^{xt}}{e^t-1}=te^{xt},
\]
and coefficient comparison gives \eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-difference}.  For
reflection,
\[
 \frac{t e^{(1-x)t}}{e^t-1}
 =\frac{(-t)e^{x(-t)}}{e^{-t}-1},
\]
which is the EGF at $x$ with $t$ replaced by $-t$.

Integrating \eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-derivative} from $0$ to $1$ gives
\[
 n\int_0^1\beta_{n-1}(x)\dd x=\beta_n(1)-\beta_n(0).
\]
The right side is zero for $n\ne1$ by
\eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-difference}; the remaining case is
$\int_0^1\beta_0(x)\dd x=1$, so after reindexing
\eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-mean-zero} follows for positive degree.

For the multiplication theorem, sum the EGFs:
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\frac{t e^{(x+r/q)t}}{e^t-1}
 &=\frac{t e^{xt}}{e^t-1}
   \frac{e^t-1}{e^{t/q}-1}\\
 &=\frac{t e^{xt}}{e^{t/q}-1}.
\end{align*}
Replace $t$ by $qt$ and compare coefficients.  This gives
$\sum_r\beta_n(x+r/q)=q^{1-n}\beta_n(qx)$, equivalent to
\eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-multiplication}.
\end{proof}

\section{Faulhaber's formula and a Stirling expansion}

\begin{theorem}[Faulhaber's formula]\label{thm:new-faulhaber}
For integers $N\ge1$ and $p\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-faulhaber}
 \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}k^p
 =\frac{\beta_{p+1}(N)-\beta_{p+1}}{p+1}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-faulhaber-expanded}
 \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}k^p
 =\frac1{p+1}\sum_{j=0}^{p}
 \binom{p+1}{j}\beta_jN^{p+1-j}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply \eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-difference} with $n=p+1$ and $x=k$:
\[
 \beta_{p+1}(k+1)-\beta_{p+1}(k)=(p+1)k^p.
\]
Summation over $k=0,\ldots,N-1$ telescopes and gives
\eqref{eq:new-faulhaber}.  The addition formula at $x=0$, $y=N$ expands
$\beta_{p+1}(N)$ and gives \eqref{eq:new-faulhaber-expanded}; the term
$j=p+1$ cancels $\beta_{p+1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli numbers from Stirling numbers]\label{thm:new-bernoulli-stirling}
For every $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-bernoulli-stirling}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \beta_n=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\frac{k!}{k+1}\stirtwo nk.}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $y=e^t-1$.  Since $t=\log(1+y)$,
\[
 \frac{t}{e^t-1}=\frac{\log(1+y)}{y}
 =\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}y^k.
\]
The Stirling EGF is
\[
 (e^t-1)^k=k!\sum_{n\ge k}\stirtwo nk\frac{t^n}{n!}.
\]
Substitute this into the preceding series and compare the coefficient of
$t^n/n!$.
\end{proof}

\section{Two modified conventions}

Some references replace only the exceptional value $\beta_1=-1/2$ by
$+1/2$.  Define $\beta_n^+$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-plus-bernoulli}
 \frac{te^t}{e^t-1}=\sum_{n\ge0}\beta_n^+\frac{t^n}{n!}.
\end{equation}
Then $\beta_1^+=1/2$ and $\beta_n^+=\beta_n$ for $n\ne1$.  This is a
notational convention, not a new sequence in positive even degree.

A different object, also called a modified Bernoulli sequence in the supplied
MathWorld material, is encoded by the logarithm of a hyperbolic sine.

\begin{proposition}[Modified Bernoulli logarithm]\label{prop:new-modified-bernoulli}
For $|z|<2\pi$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-modified-bernoulli}
 \frac12\log\left(\frac{e^{z/2}-e^{-z/2}}{z/2}\right)
 =\frac12\log2+
 \sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\beta_{2m}}{4m(2m)!}z^{2m}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the left side:
\[
 \frac14\coth\frac z2-\frac1{2z}.
\]
From the Bernoulli generating function,
\[
 \coth\frac z2=\frac2z+
 2\sum_{m\ge1}\beta_{2m}\frac{z^{2m-1}}{(2m)!}.
\]
The singular terms cancel, leaving
$\sum_{m\ge1}\beta_{2m}z^{2m-1}/(2(2m)!)$.  Integration gives the series,
and the value at $z=0$ is $\frac12\log2$.
\end{proof}

\section{Bernoulli polynomials of the second kind}

Define the Bernoulli polynomials of the second kind, also called Cauchy
polynomials in this normalization, by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-bernoulli-second-egf}
 \frac{z(1+z)^x}{\log(1+z)}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}\mathfrak b_n(x)\frac{z^n}{n!}.
\end{equation}
Their values $\mathfrak b_n=\mathfrak b_n(0)$ begin
\[
 1,\quad \frac12,\quad-\frac16,\quad\frac14,\quad-\frac{19}{30},\ldots.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Integral and difference representations]\label{thm:new-bernoulli-second}
For every $n\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak b_n(x)&=\int_x^{x+1}\falling{u}{n}\dd u,
 \label{eq:new-bernoulli-second-integral}\\
 \mathfrak b_n'(x)&=n\falling{x}{n-1}, &&n\ge1,
 \label{eq:new-bernoulli-second-derivative}\\
 \mathfrak b_n(x+1)-\mathfrak b_n(x)&=n\mathfrak b_{n-1}(x), &&n\ge1.
 \label{eq:new-bernoulli-second-difference}
\end{align}
At $x=0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-cauchy-stirling}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \mathfrak b_n=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{s(n,k)}{k+1}.}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since
\[
 \int_x^{x+1}(1+z)^u\dd u
 =\frac{z(1+z)^x}{\log(1+z)},
\]
and $(1+z)^u=\sum_{n\ge0}\falling{u}{n}z^n/n!$, coefficient comparison
gives \eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-second-integral}.  Differentiating the integral
with moving endpoints gives
\[
 \falling{x+1}{n}-\falling{x}{n}=n\falling{x}{n-1},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-second-derivative}.  Alternatively,
differentiating the EGF with respect to $x$ cancels the logarithm and gives the
same result.  Replacing $x$ by $x+1$ in the EGF multiplies it by $1+z$;
subtracting the original EGF therefore multiplies it by $z$, proving
\eqref{eq:new-bernoulli-second-difference}.  Finally,
$\falling{u}{n}=\sum_{k=0}^ns(n,k)u^k$; integrate from $0$ to $1$ to obtain
\eqref{eq:new-cauchy-stirling}.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Euler polynomials, Genocchi numbers, and alternating sums}
\label{ch:euler-genocchi}

\section{Euler polynomials}

Define the Euler polynomials $\mathcal E_n(x)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-euler-polynomial-egf}
 \frac{2e^{xt}}{e^t+1}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}\mathcal E_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}.
\end{equation}
This series is formal and is analytic for $|t|<\pi$.

\begin{theorem}[Euler-polynomial calculus]\label{thm:new-euler-basic}
For $n\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \mathcal E_n'(x)&=n\mathcal E_{n-1}(x), &&n\ge1,
 \label{eq:new-euler-derivative}\\
 \mathcal E_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk\mathcal E_k(x)y^{n-k},
 \label{eq:new-euler-addition}\\
 \mathcal E_n(x+1)+\mathcal E_n(x)&=2x^n,
 \label{eq:new-euler-difference}\\
 \mathcal E_n(1-x)&=(-1)^n\mathcal E_n(x).
 \label{eq:new-euler-reflection}
\end{align}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-euler-bernoulli-relation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \mathcal E_n(x)=\frac{2^{n+1}}{n+1}
 \left[
 \beta_{n+1}\left(\frac{x+1}{2}\right)
 -\beta_{n+1}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)
 \right].}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiation in $x$ and multiplication by $e^{yt}$ prove the first two
identities.  The sum of the EGFs at $x+1$ and $x$ is
$2e^{xt}$, proving \eqref{eq:new-euler-difference}.  Also,
\[
 \frac{2e^{(1-x)t}}{e^t+1}
 =\frac{2e^{x(-t)}}{e^{-t}+1},
\]
which proves reflection.

For the Bernoulli relation, form the EGF of its right side.  With
$m=n+1$, it is
\begin{align*}
 \frac1t\biggl(
 \sum_{m\ge0}\beta_m\left(\frac{x+1}{2}\right)
       \frac{(2t)^m}{m!}
 -\sum_{m\ge0}\beta_m\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)
       \frac{(2t)^m}{m!}\biggr)
 &=\frac{2(e^{(x+1)t}-e^{xt})}{e^{2t}-1}\\
 &=\frac{2e^{xt}}{e^t+1}.
\end{align*}
The constant terms cancel before division by $t$, so coefficient comparison is
valid and proves \eqref{eq:new-euler-bernoulli-relation}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Finite alternating power sums]\label{cor:new-alternating-powers}
For $N\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-alternating-powers}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(-1)^k(x+k)^n
 =\frac{\mathcal E_n(x)+(-1)^{N-1}\mathcal E_n(x+N)}{2}.}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Replace $x$ in \eqref{eq:new-euler-difference} by $x+k$, multiply by
$(-1)^k$, and sum.  All interior Euler-polynomial terms cancel pairwise,
leaving the two endpoints.
\end{proof}

\section{Genocchi numbers and polynomials}

Define the Genocchi polynomials $\mathsf G_n(x)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-genocchi-egf}
 \frac{2t e^{xt}}{e^t+1}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}\mathsf G_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!},
 \qquad \mathsf G_n=\mathsf G_n(0).
\end{equation}
Thus $\mathsf G_0=0$, $\mathsf G_1=1$, $\mathsf G_2=-1$,
$\mathsf G_4=1$, and $\mathsf G_6=-3$.

\begin{theorem}[Genocchi relations]\label{thm:new-genocchi}
For $n\ge1$,
\begin{align}
 \mathsf G_n(x)&=n\mathcal E_{n-1}(x),
 \label{eq:new-genocchi-euler}\\
 \mathsf G_n&=2(1-2^n)\beta_n,
 \label{eq:new-genocchi-bernoulli}\\
 \mathsf G_n(x+1)+\mathsf G_n(x)&=2n x^{n-1}.
 \label{eq:new-genocchi-difference}
\end{align}
In particular, $\mathsf G_{2m+1}=0$ for $m\ge1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Multiplying the Euler-polynomial EGF by $t$ shifts its coefficients and gives
\eqref{eq:new-genocchi-euler}.  At $x=0$,
\begin{align*}
 \frac{2t}{e^t+1}
 &=\frac{2t}{e^t-1}-\frac{4t}{e^{2t}-1}\\
 &=2\sum_{n\ge0}\beta_n\frac{t^n}{n!}
   -2\sum_{n\ge0}\beta_n\frac{(2t)^n}{n!},
\end{align*}
which proves \eqref{eq:new-genocchi-bernoulli}.  Multiplying
\eqref{eq:new-euler-difference} by $n$ and replacing $n$ there by $n-1$
gives \eqref{eq:new-genocchi-difference}.  The vanishing of positive odd
indices above $1$ follows from the corresponding Bernoulli-number vanishing.
\end{proof}

If $\tan z=\sum_{m\ge1}\mathsf T_{2m-1}z^{2m-1}/(2m-1)!$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-tangent-genocchi}
 \mathsf T_{2m-1}=(-1)^m\frac{2^{2m-2}}{m}\mathsf G_{2m}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, the standard identity
$\mathsf T_{2m-1}=(-1)^{m-1}2^{2m}(2^{2m}-1)\beta_{2m}/(2m)$ and
\eqref{eq:new-genocchi-bernoulli} are algebraically equivalent; alternatively
it follows by comparing the generating functions of $\tan z$ and the even
part of $2t/(e^t+1)$.

\chapter{Complementary Bell numbers and reciprocal partition transforms}
\label{ch:complementary-bell}

Let $T_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n\stirtwo nk x^k$ be the Touchard polynomial.
The complementary Bell number is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-complementary-bell-def}
 \BellComp n:=T_n(-1)=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\stirtwo nk.
\end{equation}
It is the signed enumeration of set partitions, where a partition with $k$
blocks has weight $(-1)^k$.

\begin{theorem}[Generating function, Dobiński formula, and recurrence]
\label{thm:new-complementary-bell}
The complementary Bell numbers satisfy
\begin{align}
 \sum_{n\ge0}\BellComp n\frac{z^n}{n!}
 &=\exp(1-e^z),
 \label{eq:new-complementary-bell-egf}\\
 \BellComp n
 &=e\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{(-1)^m m^n}{m!},
 \label{eq:new-complementary-dobinski}\\
 \BellComp{n+1}
 &=-\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk\BellComp k.
 \label{eq:new-complementary-recurrence}
\end{align}
The first values are
\[
 1,-1,0,1,1,-2,-9,-9,50,267,413,\ldots.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The Touchard EGF is $\exp(x(e^z-1))$; setting $x=-1$ gives
\eqref{eq:new-complementary-bell-egf}.  Expanding
$\exp(-e^z)$ and extracting coefficients gives
\[
 e\exp(-e^z)=e\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{(-1)^m e^{mz}}{m!},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:new-complementary-dobinski}.  If $C(z)$ denotes the
EGF in \eqref{eq:new-complementary-bell-egf}, then
$C'(z)=-e^zC(z)$.  Exponential coefficient comparison gives
\eqref{eq:new-complementary-recurrence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Bell and complementary Bell inversion]\label{cor:new-bell-complement-inverse}
For every $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-bell-complement-inverse}
 \sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk\Bell k\BellComp{n-k}=\delta_{n0}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The Bell EGF is $\exp(e^z-1)$, the reciprocal of
\eqref{eq:new-complementary-bell-egf}.  The coefficient of $z^n/n!$ in their
product is the left side.
\end{proof}

More generally, $T_n(-x)$ is the signed block enumerator with a weight $-x$
per block and EGF $\exp(-x(e^z-1))$.  This one-parameter form clarifies that
the complementary sequence is not an unrelated table but the inverse, in the
exponential-convolution group, of the ordinary Bell sequence.

\chapter{Darboux formulas, Euler--Maclaurin summation, and coefficient transfer}
\label{ch:darboux-euler-maclaurin}

\section{Darboux's generalized Taylor formula}

The name ``Darboux's formula'' is used for more than one result.  The supplied
MathWorld notebook records the following weighted Taylor identity.  It should
not be confused with the coefficient-asymptotic method proved later in this
chapter.

\begin{theorem}[Darboux's weighted Taylor identity]\label{thm:new-darboux-taylor}
Let $\phi$ be a polynomial of exact degree $n$ and let $f$ have $n+1$
continuous derivatives on the segment joining $z_0$ to $z$.  Put $h=z-z_0$
and let $\phi^{(n)}$ denote the nonzero constant $n$th derivative.  Then
\begin{align}
 f(z)=f(z_0)+\frac1{\phi^{(n)}}\biggl\{&(-1)^n h^{n+1}
 \int_0^1\phi(t)f^{(n+1)}(z_0+th)\dd t
 \notag\\
 &+\sum_{k=1}^n(-1)^{k-1}h^k
 \left[\phi^{(n-k)}(1)f^{(k)}(z)
       -\phi^{(n-k)}(0)f^{(k)}(z_0)\right]\biggr\}.
 \label{eq:new-darboux-taylor}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set $F(t)=f(z_0+th)$, so $F^{(k)}(t)=h^kf^{(k)}(z_0+th)$.  Integrate
$\int_0^1\phi(t)F^{(n+1)}(t)\dd t$ by parts $n+1$ times.  Because
$\phi^{(n+1)}=0$,
\[
 \int_0^1\phi F^{(n+1)}\dd t
 =\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^j
 \left[\phi^{(j)}F^{(n-j)}\right]_{0}^{1}.
\]
The term $j=n$ is
$(-1)^n\phi^{(n)}(F(1)-F(0))$.  Solve for $F(1)-F(0)$, replace
$j$ by $n-k$ in the remaining sum, and substitute the derivatives of $F$.
This gives exactly \eqref{eq:new-darboux-taylor}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Taylor's integral remainder]\label{cor:new-taylor-remainder}
Taking $\phi(t)=(1-t)^n$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-taylor-remainder}
 f(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{f^{(k)}(z_0)}{k!}h^k
 +\frac{h^{n+1}}{n!}\int_0^1(1-t)^n
 f^{(n+1)}(z_0+th)\dd t.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $k\le n$, the derivatives $\phi^{(n-k)}(1)$ vanish, while
$\phi^{(n-k)}(0)=(-1)^{n-k}n!/k!$ and
$\phi^{(n)}=(-1)^nn!$.  Substitute these values into
\eqref{eq:new-darboux-taylor} and simplify signs.
\end{proof}

\section{Periodic Bernoulli functions}

For $m\ne1$, let $\widetilde\beta_m(x)=\beta_m(\{x\})$, where $\{x\}$ is
the fractional part.  For $m=1$, take the periodic sawtooth equal to
$\{x\}-1/2$ away from the integers; its values at the jump points do not
affect integrals.  Between consecutive integers,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-periodic-bernoulli-derivative}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd x}\widetilde\beta_m(x)
 =m\widetilde\beta_{m-1}(x).
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Fourier series and uniform bound]\label{prop:new-periodic-bernoulli-fourier}
For $p\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-periodic-bernoulli-fourier}
 \widetilde\beta_{2p}(x)
 =(-1)^{p+1}\frac{2(2p)!}{(2\pi)^{2p}}
 \sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\cos(2\pi mx)}{m^{2p}}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-periodic-bernoulli-bound}
 \left|\widetilde\beta_{2p}(x)\right|
 \le\frac{2(2p)!\zeta(2p)}{(2\pi)^{2p}}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $p=1$, compute the Fourier coefficients of
$x^2-x+1/6$ on $[0,1]$ by two integrations by parts.  For general $p$,
repeated integration by parts and
\eqref{eq:new-periodic-bernoulli-derivative} show that the $m$th nonzero
Fourier coefficient is multiplied by
$-(2p)(2p-1)/(2\pi m)^2$ when the index increases from $2p-2$ to $2p$.
This induction gives \eqref{eq:new-periodic-bernoulli-fourier}.  Absolute
convergence for $p\ge1$ and the triangle inequality give
\eqref{eq:new-periodic-bernoulli-bound}.
\end{proof}

\section{Euler--Maclaurin summation with remainder}

\begin{theorem}[Euler--Maclaurin]\label{thm:new-euler-maclaurin}
Let $a<b$ be integers, let $p\ge1$, and let $f\in C^{2p}([a,b])$.  Then
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k=a}^{b}f(k)
={}&\int_a^bf(x)\dd x+\frac{f(a)+f(b)}2
 \notag\\
 &+\sum_{r=1}^{p}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{(2r)!}
 \left(f^{(2r-1)}(b)-f^{(2r-1)}(a)\right)+R_p,
 \label{eq:new-euler-maclaurin}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-euler-maclaurin-remainder}
 R_p=-\frac1{(2p)!}\int_a^b
 \widetilde\beta_{2p}(x)f^{(2p)}(x)\dd x.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-euler-maclaurin-bound}
 |R_p|\le
 \frac{2\zeta(2p)}{(2\pi)^{2p}}
 \int_a^b|f^{(2p)}(x)|\dd x.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On one unit interval $[k,k+1]$, integration by parts gives
\[
 \int_k^{k+1}(x-k-\tfrac12)f'(x)\dd x
 =\frac{f(k)+f(k+1)}2-\int_k^{k+1}f(x)\dd x.
\]
Summing from $k=a$ to $b-1$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-em-first-step}
 \sum_{k=a}^{b}f(k)
 =\int_a^bf(x)\dd x+\frac{f(a)+f(b)}2
 +\int_a^b\widetilde\beta_1(x)f'(x)\dd x.
\end{equation}
Integrate the last integral by parts piecewise on every unit interval, using
\eqref{eq:new-periodic-bernoulli-derivative}.  The interior endpoint terms
cancel because $\beta_m(0)=\beta_m(1)$ for $m\ne1$.  Two integrations replace
$\widetilde\beta_{2r-2}f^{(2r-2)}$ by the boundary term involving
$\beta_{2r}$ and a new integral involving
$-\widetilde\beta_{2r}f^{(2r)}$.  Iterating through $r=p$ proves
\eqref{eq:new-euler-maclaurin} and
\eqref{eq:new-euler-maclaurin-remainder}.  The bound follows immediately from
\eqref{eq:new-periodic-bernoulli-bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
When $f$ is a polynomial of degree at most $2p-1$, the remainder vanishes and
Euler--Maclaurin is exact.  Applied to $f(x)=x^m$, it reproduces Faulhaber's
formula.  Applied to $f(x)=\log x$, it produces Stirling's asymptotic series;
applied to $f(x)=x\log x$, it produces the Barnes $G$ expansion developed
later.
\end{remark}

\section{Darboux coefficient transfer}

The second common use of Darboux's name concerns coefficients controlled by a
boundary singularity.

\begin{theorem}[Darboux transfer with analytic multiplier]\label{thm:new-darboux-transfer}
Let $\rho>0$, let $H$ be analytic in $|z|<R$ for some $R>\rho$, and let
$\alpha\in\C$.  Suppose
\[
 F(z)=(1-z/\rho)^{-\alpha}H(z).
\]
For a fixed positive integer $M$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-darboux-hj}
 h_j=\frac{(-\rho)^j}{j!}H^{(j)}(\rho).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 \coeff z nF(z)
={}&\rho^{-n}\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}h_j
 \binom{n+\alpha-j-1}{n}
 \notag\\
 &+O\!\left(\rho^{-n}n^{\Re\alpha-M-1}\right),
 \qquad n\to\infty.
 \label{eq:new-darboux-transfer}
\end{align}
The generalized binomial coefficient is interpreted by analytic continuation;
when the upper parameter makes the term a polynomial, the same formula is
understood in that limiting sense.  In particular, if $H(\rho)\ne0$ and
$\alpha\notin\{0,-1,-2,\ldots\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-darboux-leading}
 \coeff z nF(z)
 \sim\frac{H(\rho)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\rho^{-n}n^{\alpha-1}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Taylor-expand $H$ at $\rho$ in the coordinate $1-z/\rho$:
\[
 H(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}h_j(1-z/\rho)^j
 +(1-z/\rho)^M H_M(z),
\]
where $H_M$ is analytic in a disk of radius greater than $\rho$.  Multiplying
by $(1-z/\rho)^{-\alpha}$ gives the finite main sum in
\eqref{eq:new-darboux-transfer}.  The exact coefficient identity
\[
 \coeff z n(1-z/\rho)^{-\gamma}
 =\rho^{-n}\binom{n+\gamma-1}{n}
 =\rho^{-n}\frac{\Gamma(n+\gamma)}
 {\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(n+1)}
\]
provides each main term.

Write $H_M(z)=\sum_{k\ge0}q_kz^k$.  Cauchy's estimate gives
$|q_k|\le C R_0^{-k}$ for some $R_0>\rho$.  Convolution with the coefficients
of $(1-z/\rho)^{M-\alpha}$ then gives
\[
 \left|\coeff z n(1-z/\rho)^{M-\alpha}H_M(z)\right|
 \le C'\rho^{-n}n^{\Re\alpha-M-1}
 \sum_{k\ge0}\left(\frac{\rho}{R_0}\right)^k(1+k)^{C''},
\]
where the finitely many terms near $k=n$ are even smaller because of the
exponential factor.  The convergent sum proves the error estimate.  Finally,
Stirling's formula for the gamma ratio gives
$\Gamma(n+\alpha)/\Gamma(n+1)\sim n^{\alpha-1}$, proving
\eqref{eq:new-darboux-leading}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Catalan asymptotics]
The Catalan generating function is
\[
 C(z)=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4z}}{2z}
 =\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{1-4z}}
 =2-2(1-4z)^{1/2}+O(1-4z).
\]
The nonanalytic term has $\rho=1/4$, $\alpha=-1/2$, and multiplier $-2$.
Since $\Gamma(-1/2)=-2\sqrt\pi$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-catalan-asymptotic}
 C_n\sim\frac{4^n}{\sqrt\pi\,n^{3/2}}.
\end{equation}
This proof also explains the universal $n^{-3/2}$ factor produced by a simple
square-root branch point.
\end{example}

\part{Pochhammer, Polygamma, and Barnes Calculus}
\label{part:special-functions}

\chapter{Pochhammer symbols and parameter differentiation}
\label{ch:pochhammer}

\section{Rising and falling factorials}

For $n\ge0$, the rising Pochhammer symbol is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-pochhammer-def}
 (a)_n=a(a+1)\cdots(a+n-1),\qquad (a)_0=1.
\end{equation}
It is related to the factorial powers used earlier by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-pochhammer-relations}
 (a)_n=\rising an=\frac{\Gamma(a+n)}{\Gamma(a)},
 \qquad
 \falling{x}{n}=(-1)^n(-x)_n.
\end{equation}
The gamma quotient is initially valid away from poles and extends
polynomially in $a$ for fixed $n$.  Expanding in powers of $a$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-pochhammer-stirling}
 (a)_n=\sum_{k=0}^n\stirone nk a^k,
\end{equation}
so unsigned first-kind Stirling numbers are precisely the monomial
coefficients of the Pochhammer polynomial.

\section{The binomial theorem and Vandermonde convolution}

\begin{theorem}[Generalized binomial series]\label{thm:new-binomial-series}
For formal $z$, or analytically for $|z|<1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-binomial-series}
 (1-z)^{-a}=\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{(a)_n}{n!}z^n.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $F(z)$ be the series on the right.  Since
$(a)_{n+1}=(a+n)(a)_n$, coefficient comparison gives
\[
 (1-z)F'(z)=aF(z),\qquad F(0)=1.
\]
The unique formal solution of this differential equation is
$(1-z)^{-a}$: logarithmic differentiation gives
$F'/F=a/(1-z)$ and the initial value fixes the multiplicative constant.
For $|z|<1$, the formal solution is the analytic branch with value $1$ at
zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Vandermonde convolution]\label{cor:new-vandermonde-pochhammer}
For $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-vandermonde-pochhammer}
 (a+b)_n=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk(a)_k(b)_{n-k}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Multiply the two binomial series for $(1-z)^{-a}$ and $(1-z)^{-b}$.
Their product is $(1-z)^{-(a+b)}$; comparison of exponential-normalized
coefficients gives the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Beta integral]\label{prop:new-pochhammer-beta}
If $\Re a>0$ and $n\ge0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-pochhammer-beta}
 \int_0^1t^{a-1}(1-t)^n\dd t
 =\frac{n!}{(a)_{n+1}}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The integral is $B(a,n+1)=\Gamma(a)\Gamma(n+1)/\Gamma(a+n+1)$.
Use $\Gamma(n+1)=n!$ and
$(a)_{n+1}=\Gamma(a+n+1)/\Gamma(a)$.
\end{proof}

\section{Parameter derivatives and complete Bell polynomials}

Let $\psi(z)=\Gamma'(z)/\Gamma(z)$ and let $\psi^{(r)}$ be its $r$th
derivative.  At points where the two terms are finite, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-delta-polygamma}
 \Delta_r(a,n)=\psi^{(r)}(a+n)-\psi^{(r)}(a).
\end{equation}
For an integer $n\ge1$, the finite-sum formula below supplies its meromorphic
continuation when the separate polygamma values have poles.

\begin{theorem}[All parameter derivatives of a Pochhammer symbol]
\label{thm:new-pochhammer-parameter-derivatives}
For $n\ge0$, $m\ge1$, and
$a\notin\{0,-1,\ldots,-n+1\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-pochhammer-parameter-derivatives}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \frac{\partial^m}{\partial a^m}(a)_n
 =(a)_n\BellC m
 \bigl(\Delta_0(a,n),\Delta_1(a,n),\ldots,
       \Delta_{m-1}(a,n)\bigr).}
\end{equation}
The right side has removable singularities at the excluded points; its unique
polynomial continuation there equals the derivative on the left.  The case
$m=0$ is the identity $\partial_a^0(a)_n=(a)_n$.  For positive integer $n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-delta-polygamma-finite}
 \Delta_r(a,n)=(-1)^r r!\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac1{(a+j)^{r+1}}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $L(a)=\log(a)_n=\log\Gamma(a+n)-\log\Gamma(a)$.  Then
$L^{(j)}(a)=\Delta_{j-1}(a,n)$ for $j\ge1$.  Since $(a)_n=e^{L(a)}$,
Faà di Bruno's formula for the exponential outer function gives
\eqref{eq:new-pochhammer-parameter-derivatives}.  The gamma recurrence implies
$\psi(z+1)-\psi(z)=1/z$.  Differentiate $r$ times and telescope from $a$ to
$a+n$ to obtain \eqref{eq:new-delta-polygamma-finite}.
\end{proof}

The first three cases are
\begin{align}
 \partial_a(a)_n
 &=(a)_n\Delta_0,
 \label{eq:new-pochhammer-first-derivative}\\
 \partial_a^2(a)_n
 &=(a)_n(\Delta_0^2+\Delta_1),
 \label{eq:new-pochhammer-second-derivative}\\
 \partial_a^3(a)_n
 &=(a)_n(\Delta_0^3+3\Delta_0\Delta_1+\Delta_2).
 \label{eq:new-pochhammer-third-derivative}
\end{align}
Thus Bell polynomials organize derivatives with respect to a hypergeometric
parameter exactly as they organize derivatives of ordinary composites.

\begin{corollary}[Derivatives of reciprocal Pochhammer symbols]
\label{cor:new-reciprocal-pochhammer}
For $m\ge1$ and $(a)_n\ne0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-reciprocal-pochhammer}
 \frac{\partial^m}{\partial a^m}\frac1{(a)_n}
 =\frac1{(a)_n}\BellC m
 \bigl(-\Delta_0,-\Delta_1,\ldots,-\Delta_{m-1}\bigr).
\end{equation}
For $m=0$ both sides are simply $(a)_n^{-1}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Write $(a)_n^{-1}=e^{-L(a)}$ and apply the same exponential form of
Faà di Bruno's formula.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Digamma and polygamma functions from first principles}
\label{ch:polygamma}

\section{Euler's limiting product for the gamma function}

\begin{theorem}[Euler limit]\label{thm:new-euler-gamma-limit}
For $\Re z>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-euler-gamma-limit}
 \Gamma(z)=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{N!\,N^z}{z(z+1)\cdots(z+N)},
\end{equation}
locally uniformly in $z$ on the right half-plane.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The beta integral gives
\[
 B(z,N+1)=\int_0^1t^{z-1}(1-t)^N\dd t
 =\frac{N!}{z(z+1)\cdots(z+N)}.
\]
After multiplying by $N^z$ and substituting $u=Nt$,
\[
 N^zB(z,N+1)=\int_0^N u^{z-1}\left(1-\frac uN\right)^N\dd u.
\]
For $0\le u\le N$, $(1-u/N)^N\le e^{-u}$.  On compact subsets of
$\Re z>0$, the integrand is dominated by an integrable multiple of
$u^{\sigma-1}e^{-u}$ near zero and infinity.  It converges pointwise to
$u^{z-1}e^{-u}$, so dominated convergence, uniformly on compact subsets,
gives the gamma integral.
\end{proof}

\section{Series for the logarithmic derivatives}

Let $\gamma$ be Euler's constant.

\begin{theorem}[Digamma and polygamma series]\label{thm:new-polygamma-series}
For $z\notin\{0,-1,-2,\ldots\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-digamma-series}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \psi(z)=-\gamma+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
 \left(\frac1{k+1}-\frac1{k+z}\right).}
\end{equation}
For $m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-polygamma-series}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \psi^{(m)}(z)=(-1)^{m+1}m!
 \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac1{(k+z)^{m+1}}.}
\end{equation}
Both series converge locally uniformly away from the poles.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Take the logarithmic derivative of the finite expression in
\eqref{eq:new-euler-gamma-limit}:
\[
 \frac{\dd}{\dd z}\log\left(
 \frac{N!N^z}{z(z+1)\cdots(z+N)}\right)
 =\log N-\sum_{k=0}^N\frac1{k+z}.
\]
Rewrite the right side as
\[
 \bigl(\log N-H_{N+1}\bigr)
 +\sum_{k=0}^N\left(\frac1{k+1}-\frac1{k+z}\right).
\]
The first term tends to $-\gamma$.  On compact subsets of the right
half-plane, the Euler approximants converge locally uniformly to the nonvanishing
function $\Gamma$; Cauchy's integral formula therefore gives convergence of
their logarithmic derivatives.  The summand in the second term is $O(k^{-2})$
locally uniformly in $z$, so the limit may be summed termwise.  This proves
\eqref{eq:new-digamma-series} first for $\Re z>0$.  The series on the right
converges locally uniformly on
$\C\setminus\{0,-1,-2,\ldots\}$ and defines a meromorphic function there;
analytic continuation gives the asserted identity throughout that domain.
Termwise differentiation, justified locally uniformly away from the poles, gives
\eqref{eq:new-polygamma-series}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Recurrences and integer values]\label{cor:new-polygamma-integers}
For $m\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \psi(z+1)&=\psi(z)+\frac1z,
 \label{eq:new-digamma-recurrence}\\
 \psi^{(m)}(z+1)&=\psi^{(m)}(z)
 +(-1)^m\frac{m!}{z^{m+1}}.
 \label{eq:new-polygamma-recurrence}
\end{align}
For $n\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \psi(n+1)&=H_n-\gamma,
 \label{eq:new-digamma-integer}\\
 \psi^{(m)}(n+1)&=(-1)^{m+1}m!
 \left(\zeta(m+1)-H_n^{(m+1)}\right),\qquad m\ge1.
 \label{eq:new-polygamma-integer}
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Subtract the series at $z$ from the series at $z+1$; all terms telescope.
Starting from $\psi(1)=-\gamma$ and
$\psi^{(m)}(1)=(-1)^{m+1}m!\zeta(m+1)$ and iterating the recurrences gives
the integer values.
\end{proof}

\section{Reflection and multiplication}

\begin{theorem}[Reflection]\label{thm:new-digamma-reflection}
For $z\notin\Z$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-digamma-reflection}
 \psi(1-z)-\psi(z)=\pi\cot(\pi z).
\end{equation}
More generally, for $m\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-polygamma-reflection}
 (-1)^m\psi^{(m)}(1-z)-\psi^{(m)}(z)
 =\frac{\dd^m}{\dd z^m}\bigl(\pi\cot(\pi z)\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Subtract the two digamma series:
\[
 \psi(1-z)-\psi(z)
 =\frac1z+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \left(\frac1{k+z}-\frac1{k-z}\right).
\]
The Weierstrass product
$\sin(\pi z)=\pi z\prod_{k\ge1}(1-z^2/k^2)$ has locally uniform logarithmic
derivative
\[
 \pi\cot(\pi z)=\frac1z+
 \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac1{k+z}-\frac1{k-z}\right),
\]
which proves the first formula.  Repeated differentiation gives the second.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Multiplication]\label{thm:new-polygamma-multiplication}
For a positive integer $q$,
\begin{align}
 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\psi\left(z+\frac rq\right)
 &=q\psi(qz)-q\log q,
 \label{eq:new-digamma-multiplication}\\
 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\psi^{(m)}\left(z+\frac rq\right)
 &=q^{m+1}\psi^{(m)}(qz),\qquad m\ge1.
 \label{eq:new-polygamma-multiplication}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $m\ge1$, substitute \eqref{eq:new-polygamma-series} and write every
nonnegative integer uniquely as $qk+r$:
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\sum_{k\ge0}
 \left(z+\frac rq+k\right)^{-m-1}
 &=q^{m+1}\sum_{j\ge0}(qz+j)^{-m-1}.
\end{align*}
This proves \eqref{eq:new-polygamma-multiplication}.  The derivative with
respect to $z$ of the difference between the two sides of
\eqref{eq:new-digamma-multiplication} is zero by the case $m=1$, so that
difference is constant.  As $z\to+\infty$, the Euler--Maclaurin estimate
$\psi(z)=\log z+O(1/z)$ shows that the constant is $-q\log q$.
\end{proof}

For $x>0$, \eqref{eq:new-polygamma-series} also gives the sign law
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-polygamma-sign}
 (-1)^{m+1}\psi^{(m)}(x)>0\qquad(m\ge1),
\end{equation}
which is frequently useful when proving convexity of gamma and Pochhammer
ratios.

\chapter{Barnes' \texorpdfstring{$G$}{G}-function and superfactorial asymptotics}
\label{ch:barnes-g}

\section{Canonical product and recurrence}

Barnes' $G$-function is the entire function defined by the canonical product
of Barnes \cite{Barnes1900}; the normalization and asymptotic conventions below
agree with \cite{NISTDLMF}.
\begin{align}
 G(z+1)=&(2\pi)^{z/2}
 \exp\left(-\frac{z+(1+\gamma)z^2}{2}\right)
 \notag\\
 &\times\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \left\{\left(1+\frac zk\right)^k
 \exp\left(\frac{z^2}{2k}-z\right)\right\}.
 \label{eq:new-barnes-product}
\end{align}
The logarithm of the $k$th factor is $O(k^{-2})$ uniformly on compact
$z$-sets, so the product converges locally uniformly and defines an entire
function.

\begin{theorem}[Barnes recurrence]\label{thm:new-barnes-recurrence}
The product \eqref{eq:new-barnes-product} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-barnes-recurrence}
 G(z+1)=\Gamma(z)G(z),\qquad G(1)=1.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for integers $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-barnes-integers}
 G(n+1)=\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}k!
 =\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}j^{n-j}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $P(z)$ denote the right side of \eqref{eq:new-barnes-product}, so that
$P(z)=G(z+1)$, and let $P_N$ be the product truncated after $k=N$.  Setting
$z=0$ gives $P(0)=1$.  Direct division of the two truncated products gives
\begin{align*}
 \frac{P_N(z)}{P_N(z-1)}
 ={}&\sqrt{2\pi}
 \exp\left(-(1+\gamma)z+\frac\gamma2\right)
 \prod_{k=1}^N
 \left(\frac{k+z}{k+z-1}\right)^k
 \exp\left(\frac{2z-1}{2k}-1\right).
\end{align*}
The algebraic product telescopes:
\begin{align*}
 \prod_{k=1}^N\left(\frac{k+z}{k+z-1}\right)^k
 &=\frac{(N+z)^N}{z\prod_{k=1}^{N-1}(k+z)}
 =\Gamma(z)\frac{(N+z)^N}{\Gamma(N+z)}.
\end{align*}
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-barnes-ratio-finite}
 \frac{P_N(z)}{P_N(z-1)}
 =\Gamma(z)A_N(z),
\end{equation}
where
\[
 A_N(z)=\sqrt{2\pi}
 e^{-(1+\gamma)z+\gamma/2}
 \frac{(N+z)^N}{\Gamma(N+z)}
 e^{(z-1/2)H_N-N}.
\]
The classical estimates
$H_N=\log N+\gamma+O(N^{-1})$ and
\[
 \Gamma(N+z)=\sqrt{2\pi}
 (N+z)^{N+z-1/2}e^{-(N+z)}(1+O(N^{-1}))
\]
hold locally uniformly for bounded $z$.  Substitution shows
\[
 A_N(z)=
 \left(\frac{N}{N+z}\right)^{z-1/2}(1+O(N^{-1}))\longrightarrow1;
\]
all constant exponential factors cancel exactly.  Local uniform convergence of
the canonical products now lets $N\to\infty$ in
\eqref{eq:new-barnes-ratio-finite}, yielding
$P(z)/P(z-1)=\Gamma(z)$ away from the poles; analytic continuation gives the
functional equation everywhere it is defined.  Since $P(0)=1$, this is
$G(z+1)=\Gamma(z)G(z)$ with $G(1)=1$.

Iteration gives
$G(n+1)=\Gamma(n)\cdots\Gamma(1)=\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}k!$.  A fixed integer
$j$ occurs in $j!, (j+1)!,\ldots,(n-1)!$, exactly $n-j$ times, giving the
second product in \eqref{eq:new-barnes-integers}.
\end{proof}

Termwise logarithmic differentiation of the canonical product gives the
convergent representation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-barnes-log-derivative}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd z}\log G(z+1)
 =\frac12\log(2\pi)-\frac12-(1+\gamma)z
 +\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \left(\frac{k}{k+z}-1+\frac zk\right).
\end{equation}
It is the order-two analogue of the digamma series.

\section{Asymptotic expansion}

\begin{theorem}[Barnes asymptotic expansion on the positive integers]
\label{thm:new-barnes-asymptotic}
For each fixed $p\ge1$, as $N\to\infty$ through positive integers,
\begin{align}
 \log G(N+1)
={}&\frac{N^2}{2}\log N-\frac{3N^2}{4}
 +\frac N2\log(2\pi)-\frac1{12}\log N+\zeta'(-1)
 \notag\\
 &+\sum_{r=1}^{p-1}
 \frac{\beta_{2r+2}}{2r(2r+2)N^{2r}}
 +O(N^{-2p}).
 \label{eq:new-barnes-asymptotic}
\end{align}
Equivalently, if $A$ is the Glaisher--Kinkelin constant, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-glaisher-zeta}
 \log A=\frac1{12}-\zeta'(-1).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
From \eqref{eq:new-barnes-integers},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-barnes-sum}
 \log G(N+1)=N\log\Gamma(N)-\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}k\log k.
\end{equation}
Apply Euler--Maclaurin to $\log x$ and to $x\log x$.  The first application
gives Stirling's expansion
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-stirling-for-barnes}
 \log\Gamma(N)=
 \left(N-\frac12\right)\log N-N+\frac12\log(2\pi)
 +\sum_{r=1}^{p}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{2r(2r-1)N^{2r-1}}
 +O(N^{-2p-1}).
\end{equation}
For the second sum, apply Euler--Maclaurin to the tail of
$\zeta(s)=\sum_{k\ge1}k^{-s}$, initially for $\Re s>1$ and then by analytic
continuation.  Uniformly for $s$ near $-1$,
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{k=1}^{N-1}k^{-s}
 ={}&\zeta(s)-\frac{N^{1-s}}{s-1}-\frac12N^{-s}\\
 &-\sum_{r=1}^{p+1}
 \frac{\beta_{2r}}{(2r)!}(s)_{2r-1}N^{1-s-2r}
 +O(N^{-\Re s-2p-1}),
\end{align*}
with the same bound after one $s$-derivative (up to a harmless logarithm that
is absorbed after retaining the next term).  Since
\[
 -\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
 \sum_{k=1}^{N-1}k^{-s}\right|_{s=-1}
 =\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}k\log k,
\]
differentiate the displayed continuation at $s=-1$.  The first three tail
terms contribute, respectively,
\[
 \frac{N^2}{2}\log N-\frac{N^2}{4},\qquad
 -\frac N2\log N,\qquad
 \frac1{12}\log N+\frac1{12}.
\]
For $r\ge2$, $(s)_{2r-1}$ contains the single factor $s+1$ and
\[
 \left.\frac{\dd}{\dd s}(s)_{2r-1}\right|_{s=-1}
 =-(2r-3)!.
\]
Reindexing $r\mapsto r+1$ therefore gives
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k=1}^{N-1}k\log k
={}&\frac{N^2}{2}\log N-\frac{N^2}{4}
 -\frac N2\log N+\frac1{12}\log N
 \notag\\
 &+\frac1{12}-\zeta'(-1)
 +\sum_{r=1}^{p-1}d_rN^{-2r}+O(N^{-2p}),
 \label{eq:new-hyperfactorial-expansion}
\end{align}
where
\[
 d_r=-\frac{\beta_{2r+2}}
 {2r(2r+1)(2r+2)}.
\]
Substitute \eqref{eq:new-stirling-for-barnes} and
\eqref{eq:new-hyperfactorial-expansion} into
\eqref{eq:new-barnes-sum}.  The $N\log N$ terms cancel, the constants
$1/12$ cancel, and the two Bernoulli contributions combine to
$\beta_{2r+2}/(2r(2r+2))$.  This yields
\eqref{eq:new-barnes-asymptotic}.  The Euler--Maclaurin remainder bound from
\Cref{thm:new-euler-maclaurin} gives the stated error after the two
applications.

The traditional constant $A$ is defined by the hyperfactorial limit
\[
 A=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{\prod_{k=1}^{N-1}k^k}
 {N^{N^2/2-N/2+1/12}e^{-N^2/4}}.
\]
Taking logarithms in \eqref{eq:new-hyperfactorial-expansion} identifies its
constant as $1/12-\zeta'(-1)$, proving
\eqref{eq:new-glaisher-zeta}.
\end{proof}

The first correction is
\[
 \frac{\beta_4}{2\cdot4N^2}=-\frac1{240N^2},
\]
so the expansion begins
\begin{align*}
 \log G(N+1)=&\frac{N^2}{2}\log N-\frac{3N^2}{4}
 +\frac N2\log(2\pi)-\frac1{12}\log N+\zeta'(-1)\\
 &-\frac1{240N^2}+\frac1{1008N^4}+O(N^{-6}).
\end{align*}
The Bernoulli coefficients therefore link Barnes' superfactorial growth
directly to the same summation calculus that controls Stirling's expansion for
$\Gamma$.

\part{Extended Inversion and Analytic Applications}
\label{part:extended-inversion}

\chapter{Inverse functions beyond the basic examples}
\label{ch:extended-inverse-functions}

\section{Inverse trigonometric and hyperbolic series}

\begin{theorem}[Four classical inverse series]\label{thm:new-four-inverse-series}
For $|y|<1$,
\begin{align}
 \arcsin y
 &=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac1{2m+1}\binom{2m}{m}
   \frac{y^{2m+1}}{4^m},
 \label{eq:new-arcsin-series}\\
 \arctan y
 &=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{(-1)^m}{2m+1}y^{2m+1},
 \label{eq:new-arctan-series}\\
 \operatorname{arsinh}y
 &=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{(-1)^m}{2m+1}\binom{2m}{m}
   \frac{y^{2m+1}}{4^m},
 \label{eq:new-arsinh-series}\\
 \operatorname{artanh}y
 &=\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{y^{2m+1}}{2m+1}.
 \label{eq:new-artanh-series}
\end{align}
Consequently, Lagrange inversion gives the coefficient evaluations
\begin{align}
 \coeff x{2m}\left(\frac{x}{\sin x}\right)^{2m+1}
 &=\frac1{4^m}\binom{2m}{m},
 \label{eq:new-sine-diagonal}\\
 \coeff x{2m}\left(\frac{x}{\tan x}\right)^{2m+1}
 &=(-1)^m,
 \label{eq:new-tangent-diagonal}\\
 \coeff x{2m}\left(\frac{x}{\sinh x}\right)^{2m+1}
 &=(-1)^m\frac1{4^m}\binom{2m}{m},
 \label{eq:new-sinh-diagonal}\\
 \coeff x{2m}\left(\frac{x}{\tanh x}\right)^{2m+1}
 &=1.
 \label{eq:new-tanh-diagonal}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The derivatives of the four inverse functions are, respectively,
\[
 (1-y^2)^{-1/2},\qquad (1+y^2)^{-1},\qquad
 (1+y^2)^{-1/2},\qquad (1-y^2)^{-1}.
\]
Expand them by the generalized binomial theorem or the geometric series and
integrate term by term from $0$ to $y$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:new-arcsin-series}--\eqref{eq:new-artanh-series}.

If $g=f^{\langle-1\rangle}$ with $f(0)=0$ and $f'(0)=1$, Lagrange inversion
says
\[
 \coeff y n g(y)=\frac1n\coeff x{n-1}
 \left(\frac{x}{f(x)}\right)^n.
\]
Apply this with $f=\sin,\tan,\sinh,\tanh$, take $n=2m+1$, and compare with
the four explicit inverse coefficients.
\end{proof}

\section{Diagonal identities for generalized Bernoulli and Cauchy series}

\begin{proposition}[Exponential--logarithmic diagonal identities]
\label{prop:new-exp-log-diagonals}
For $n\ge1$,
\begin{align}
 \coeff x{n-1}\left(\frac{x}{e^x-1}\right)^n
 &=(-1)^{n-1},
 \label{eq:new-norlund-diagonal}\\
 \coeff x{n-1}\left(\frac{x}{\log(1+x)}\right)^n
 &=\frac1{(n-1)!}.
 \label{eq:new-cauchy-diagonal}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The inverse of $e^x-1$ is $\log(1+y)$, whose coefficient of $y^n$ is
$(-1)^{n-1}/n$.  Lagrange inversion therefore gives
\[
 \frac1n\coeff x{n-1}\left(\frac{x}{e^x-1}\right)^n
 =\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}n.
\]
This proves the first identity.  Conversely, the inverse of $\log(1+x)$ is
$e^y-1$, whose coefficient of $y^n$ is $1/n!$.  A second application gives
the second identity.
\end{proof}

\section{Implicit equations with an offset}

\begin{theorem}[Offset Lagrange--Bürmann formula]\label{thm:new-offset-lagrange}
Suppose $u(t)$ is determined by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-offset-equation}
 u=a+t\Phi(u),\qquad \Phi(a)\ne0.
\end{equation}
Then for $n\ge1$ and any formal or analytic $H$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-offset-lagrange}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \coeff t n H(u(t))
 =\frac1n\coeff z{n-1}H'(a+z)\Phi(a+z)^n.}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $w=u-a$ and $\phi(w)=\Phi(a+w)$.  Then $w=t\phi(w)$ and
$H(u)=\widetilde H(w)$ with $\widetilde H(w)=H(a+w)$.  The ordinary
Lagrange--Bürmann formula gives
\[
 \coeff t n\widetilde H(w(t))
 =\frac1n\coeff z{n-1}\widetilde H'(z)\phi(z)^n,
\]
which is exactly \eqref{eq:new-offset-lagrange}.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Narayana refinement of the Catalan family}
\label{ch:narayana}

The Catalan material in the supplemental Wikipedia files admits a useful
bivariate refinement.  Let $N(n,k)$ count Dyck paths of semilength $n$ with
exactly $k$ peaks.

\begin{theorem}[Narayana numbers]\label{thm:new-narayana}
For $1\le k\le n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-narayana}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 N(n,k)=\frac1n\binom nk\binom n{k-1}.}
\end{equation}
Thus $\sum_{k=1}^nN(n,k)=C_n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $C(z,u)$ be the generating function in which $z$ marks semilength and
$u$ marks peaks, including the empty path.  A nonempty Dyck path decomposes
uniquely as $UADB$, where $A$ and $B$ are Dyck paths.  If $A$ is empty, the
initial $UD$ is a new peak; if $A$ is nonempty, it is not.  Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-narayana-functional}
 C=1+zC(C+u-1).
\end{equation}
Put $W=C-1$.  Then
$W=z(1+W)(u+W)$.  Lagrange inversion gives
\begin{align*}
 [z^nu^k]W
 &=\frac1n[w^{n-1}u^k](1+w)^n(u+w)^n\\
 &=\frac1n\binom nk\binom n{k-1},
\end{align*}
which proves \eqref{eq:new-narayana}.  Setting $u=1$ makes
\eqref{eq:new-narayana-functional} the Catalan equation $C=1+zC^2$, so the
row sum is $C_n$.
\end{proof}

The Narayana polynomial $\sum_kN(n,k)u^k$ is symmetric because
$N(n,k)=N(n,n+1-k)$.  In the standard geometric interpretation, the same row
is the $h$-vector of the associahedron, linking the bivariate Catalan
refinement to the face structure already used in associahedral inversion.

\chapter{Good's multivariate inversion formula}
\label{ch:good-inversion}

Let $\boldsymbol w=(w_1,\ldots,w_d)$ be the unique formal solution of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-good-system}
 w_i=t_i\phi_i(\boldsymbol w),\qquad \phi_i(\boldsymbol0)\ne0,
 \quad 1\le i\le d.
\end{equation}
For a multi-index $\boldsymbol n=(n_1,\ldots,n_d)$, write
$\boldsymbol t^{\boldsymbol n}=\prod_i t_i^{n_i}$ and
$\boldsymbol\phi^{\boldsymbol n}=\prod_i\phi_i^{n_i}$.

\begin{theorem}[Good's formula \cite{Good1960}]\label{thm:new-good}
For every formal series $F$ and every
$\boldsymbol n\in\N^d$,
\begin{align}
 [\boldsymbol t^{\boldsymbol n}]F(\boldsymbol w(\boldsymbol t))
={}&[\boldsymbol x^{\boldsymbol n}]
 F(\boldsymbol x)\boldsymbol\phi(\boldsymbol x)^{\boldsymbol n}
 \notag\\
 &\times\det\left(
 \delta_{ij}-\frac{x_j}{\phi_i(\boldsymbol x)}
 \frac{\partial\phi_i}{\partial x_j}(\boldsymbol x)
 \right)_{i,j=1}^{d}.
 \label{eq:new-good}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Represent the coefficient on the left by an iterated formal residue:
\[
 \operatorname*{Res}_{\boldsymbol t}
 F(\boldsymbol w(\boldsymbol t))
 \prod_{i=1}^dt_i^{-n_i-1}\dd t_1\cdots\dd t_d.
\]
Make the formal change of variables
$t_i=x_i/\phi_i(\boldsymbol x)$.  Its Jacobian has entries
\[
 \frac{\partial t_i}{\partial x_j}
 =\frac1{\phi_i}
 \left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{x_i}{\phi_i}
 \frac{\partial\phi_i}{\partial x_j}\right).
\]
Because \eqref{eq:new-good-system} implies
$\boldsymbol w(\boldsymbol t(\boldsymbol x))=\boldsymbol x$, the transformed
residue is
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname*{Res}_{\boldsymbol x}
 F(\boldsymbol x)
 \prod_i\phi_i(\boldsymbol x)^{n_i}x_i^{-n_i-1}
 \det\left(
 \delta_{ij}-\frac{x_i}{\phi_i}
 \frac{\partial\phi_i}{\partial x_j}
 \right)\dd\boldsymbol x.
\end{align*}
Let $X=\diag(x_1,\ldots,x_d)$ and
$Y=\diag(\phi_1^{-1},\ldots,\phi_d^{-1})J_\phi$.
The determinant displayed here is $\det(I-XY)$.  The identity
$\det(I-XY)=\det(I-YX)$ changes it into the determinant in
\eqref{eq:new-good}, whose $(i,j)$ entry contains $x_j$.  Reading the final
residue as a coefficient proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
For $d=1$ the determinant is $1-x\phi'(x)/\phi(x)$.  Formal integration by
parts reduces Good's formula to the one-variable Lagrange--Bürmann theorem.
The determinant is therefore the genuine multivariate Jacobian correction,
not an optional decoration.
\end{remark}

\chapter{Kepler's equation and Fourier--Bessel reversion}
\label{ch:kepler}

For eccentricity $0\le e<1$, Kepler's equation is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-kepler}
 E=M+e\sin E.
\end{equation}
The shifted reversion theorem immediately gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-kepler-reversion}
 E=M+\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{e^k}{k!}
 \left(\frac{\dd}{\dd M}\right)^{k-1}\sin^kM.
\end{equation}
The same solution has a global Fourier expansion.

\begin{theorem}[Fourier--Bessel solution of Kepler's equation]
\label{thm:new-kepler-bessel}
For $0\le e<1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-kepler-bessel}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 E=M+2\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{J_n(ne)}n\sin(nM),}
\end{equation}
where $J_n$ is the Bessel function of the first kind.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The map $E\mapsto M=E-e\sin E$ has derivative $1-e\cos E>0$, so it is
strictly increasing and its inverse satisfies $E(M+2\pi)=E(M)+2\pi$.
Thus $q(M)=E(M)-M$ is a smooth $2\pi$-periodic odd function.  Let $c_n$ be
its sine coefficient.  Since $q=e\sin E$ and
$\dd M=(1-e\cos E)\dd E$,
\begin{align*}
 c_n
 &=\frac1\pi\int_0^{2\pi}q(M)\sin(nM)\dd M\\
 &=\frac e\pi\int_0^{2\pi}\sin E
 \sin\bigl(n(E-e\sin E)\bigr)(1-e\cos E)\dd E.
\end{align*}
Now
\[
 \frac{\dd}{\dd E}\cos\bigl(n(E-e\sin E)\bigr)
 =-n(1-e\cos E)\sin\bigl(n(E-e\sin E)\bigr).
\]
Integration by parts, with zero boundary term, gives
\[
 c_n=\frac e{\pi n}\int_0^{2\pi}
 \cos E\cos(nE-ne\sin E)\dd E.
\]
Using $2\cos E\cos A=\cos(A+E)+\cos(A-E)$ and the integral representation
\[
 J_m(t)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}\cos(mE-t\sin E)\dd E,
\]
we obtain
\[
 c_n=\frac e n\bigl(J_{n-1}(ne)+J_{n+1}(ne)\bigr).
\]
The Bessel recurrence
$J_{n-1}(t)+J_{n+1}(t)=2nJ_n(t)/t$ gives $c_n=2J_n(ne)/n$.
The smoothness of $q$ justifies Fourier reconstruction and proves
\eqref{eq:new-kepler-bessel}.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Critical values and square-root inverse singularities}
\label{ch:inverse-singularities}

\begin{theorem}[Local square-root form]\label{thm:new-square-root-inverse}
Let $f$ be analytic near $a$, with
\[
 f(a)=b,\qquad f'(a)=0,\qquad f''(a)\ne0.
\]
Then the two local inverse branches near $b$ have convergent Puiseux expansions
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-square-root-inverse}
 z=a+\sum_{m\ge1}c_m(w-b)^{m/2},
\end{equation}
with opposite signs for $c_1$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-square-root-leading}
 c_1=\pm\sqrt{\frac{2}{f''(a)}}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $z=a+u$.  Taylor's theorem factors
\[
 f(a+u)-b=u^2h(u),\qquad h(0)=\frac12f''(a)\ne0,
\]
with $h$ analytic.  Choose an analytic square root of $h$ near zero and put
$v=u\sqrt{h(u)}$.  Then $v'(0)=\sqrt{h(0)}\ne0$, so the analytic inverse
function theorem gives an analytic inverse $u=\chi(v)$.  Since
$v^2=w-b$, the two choices $v=\pm\sqrt{w-b}$ give
$z=a+\chi(\pm\sqrt{w-b})$, a convergent series in half-integer powers.
The linear term is $u=v/\sqrt{h(0)}+O(v^2)$, proving
\eqref{eq:new-square-root-leading}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Coefficient signature of a simple critical point]
\label{cor:new-square-root-coefficients}
Suppose one branch in \eqref{eq:new-square-root-inverse} has its unique
dominant singularity at $w=\rho$, locally contains a nonzero term
$\lambda(1-w/\rho)^{1/2}$, and its remaining local expansion satisfies the
analytic-multiplier hypotheses of \Cref{thm:new-darboux-transfer}.  Then its
Taylor coefficients have leading form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:new-square-root-coefficients}
 [w^n]z(w)\sim-\frac{\lambda}{2\sqrt\pi}\rho^{-n}n^{-3/2}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply the Darboux transfer theorem with $\alpha=-1/2$ and use
$\Gamma(-1/2)=-2\sqrt\pi$.
\end{proof}

This principle explains several radii and coefficient exponents at once.
For $f(z)=ze^z$, the critical point $z=-1$ maps to $-e^{-1}$ and produces
the square-root branch of Lambert $W$.  The Catalan solution has its quadratic
critical value at $1/4$.  The inverse sine meets critical points of $\sin z$
at $\pm\pi/2$, whose images are $\pm1$.  Formal inversion determines all
coefficients without convergence assumptions; analytic continuation and the
nearest singular or critical value determine their eventual size.

\chapter{Online research update and connections}
\label{ch:online-update}

The online search used for this edition was restricted, for technical claims,
to primary or authoritative sources.  The current NIST Digital Library of
Mathematical Functions chapters on Bernoulli and Euler polynomials, gamma and
polygamma functions, and Barnes' $G$-function supplied a normalization check
for the special-function chapters \cite{NISTDLMF}.  Gessel's survey of Lagrange
inversion provided a modern cross-check on the formal-residue and multivariate
forms \cite{Gessel2016}, while Good's original paper was used for the determinant
normalization in \Cref{thm:new-good} \cite{Good1960}.  A 2026 paper by Hwang,
Li, and Zacharovas develops elementary local and central limit theorems for
second-kind Stirling numbers and effective Bell-number asymptotics
\cite{HwangLiZacharovas2026}.  Its results are compatible with the saddle-point
and real-rootedness framework proved earlier; it is recorded as a recent
continuation rather than silently folded into older attribution.

The additions in this part show three broader links that were not fully
articulated in any one draft:
\begin{enumerate}
\item inverse-function coefficients, Narayana refinements, and Abel
      polynomials are all direct Lagrange extractions;
\item a simple critical point of the forward map forces a square-root
      singularity of the inverse and hence an $n^{-3/2}$ coefficient factor;
\item Good's determinant is the multivariate Jacobian counterpart of the
      one-variable integration-by-parts term.
\end{enumerate}

\part{Source Merge, Deduplication, and Reference Record}
\label{part:source-record}

\chapter{Five-draft merge and deduplication ledger}
\label{ch:merge-ledger}

The supplied archive contains five substantial LaTeX drafts rather than five
independent topics.  Each draft covers the same central families---Stirling,
Bell, Eulerian, Faà di Bruno, and Lagrange formulae---but emphasizes different
proofs, applications, or normalizations.  A literal concatenation would repeat
most definitions and master theorems several times.  The present article uses a
theorem-level merge: one canonical statement and proof is retained for each
result, while genuinely distinct applications, algorithms, examples, and proof
methods are integrated at the point where they become mathematically natural.

\section{Deduplication rules}

The merge followed five explicit rules.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{One definition per object.}  Signed and unsigned first-kind
      Stirling numbers, ordinary and exponential Bell polynomials, Bell versus
      Bernoulli notation, and type-$B$ Eulerian indexing are fixed once and used
      throughout.
\item \emph{One master proof, many corollaries.}  The exponential formula,
      finite differences, Faà di Bruno, formal residues, and
      Lagrange--Bürmann inversion are proved once.  Repeated formulas are then
      stated as specializations rather than re-proved under new symbols.
\item \emph{Distinct proof methods are retained only when they add information.}
      For example, Kepler's equation keeps both the local reversion series and
      the global Fourier--Bessel resummation; Bell-number asymptotics keeps
      elementary bounds and saddle-point analysis because they answer different
      questions.
\item \emph{Computational claims receive certificates.}  Finite tables,
      modular searches, and low-order inverse coefficients are accompanied by
      exact recurrences or direct substitution checks.
\item \emph{Schematic statements are repaired, not silently copied.}  Missing
      ranges, index shifts, suppressed Jacobians, undefined asymptotic
      coefficients, and formal-versus-analytic ambiguities are made explicit.
\end{enumerate}

\section{MathWorld directory}

The MathWorld PDFs were accompanied by Mathematica notebook source, allowing
formula-level inspection without depending on raster extraction.  The material
added beyond the five drafts includes:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Bernoulli polynomials, the positive-$B_1$ convention, the hyperbolic-sine
      modified Bernoulli logarithm, and Bernoulli polynomials of the second
      kind;
\item Euler polynomials, Genocchi numbers and polynomials, and alternating
      power sums;
\item complementary Bell numbers, their Dobiński series, recurrence, and
      reciprocal Bell transform;
\item Darboux's weighted Taylor formula, Euler--Maclaurin summation with an
      explicit periodic-Bernoulli remainder, and the distinct Darboux
      coefficient-transfer theorem;
\item Pochhammer symbols, all parameter derivatives through Bell polynomials,
      digamma and polygamma series, and Barnes' $G$-function with its
      superfactorial asymptotic expansion.
\end{enumerate}
All of these items are derived in
\Cref{part:classical-polynomials,part:special-functions}; notebook output was
used as a discovery aid, not as a substitute for proof.

\section{Additional online research}

Current online verification used authoritative or primary sources.  The NIST
Digital Library of Mathematical Functions supplied current normalization and
asymptotic checks for Bernoulli and Euler polynomials, polygamma functions, and
Barnes' $G$-function \cite{NISTDLMF}.  Gessel's survey supplied a modern
reference for the formal and combinatorial forms of Lagrange inversion
\cite{Gessel2016}, and Good's original article fixed the multivariate determinant
normalization \cite{Good1960}.  The 2026 work of Hwang, Li, and Zacharovas was
added to the asymptotic bibliography as a recent extension of central-range
Stirling analysis \cite{HwangLiZacharovas2026}.

\chapter{Correction and proof-completeness record}
\label{ch:correction-record}

\section{Normalization corrections}

The following source-level ambiguities are resolved globally.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $s(n,k)$ is signed and $\stirone nk$ is unsigned.  They are never
      interchanged inside a logarithmic generating function or a factorial
      expansion.
\item Bell numbers are $\Bell n$, partial and complete exponential Bell
      polynomials are $\BellP nk$ and $\BellC n$, Bernoulli numbers are
      $\beta_n$, Euler polynomials are $\mathcal E_n(x)$, and complementary
      Bell numbers are $\BellComp n$.
\item Type-$B$ Eulerian numbers count descents in positions
      $0,1,\ldots,n-1$ with $\pi_0=0$.  This convention makes the corrected
      explicit formula agree with the recurrence and printed rows.
\item Ordinary and exponential inverse coefficients are separated by their
      factorial normalization; the associahedral face formula is stated for
      an ordinary series.
\item Good's determinant includes the full Jacobian.  The delta-function
      reversion proof explicitly records orientation and change-of-variables
      assumptions.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Proof-completeness policy}

A displayed identity is treated in one of four ways: proved immediately;
derived as a named corollary of an earlier proved theorem; marked explicitly as
a historical or bibliographic fact; or supplied with an exact finite
certificate algorithm.  In particular, formulas newly imported from the
supplemental directories are not left as unexplained tables.  Their proofs use
one of the reusable mechanisms established in the opening chapters:
coefficient extraction, finite differences, labelled decomposition, Möbius
inversion, repeated integration by parts, formal residues, or
Euler--Maclaurin summation.

\section{Implementation-neutral reproducibility}

Every triangular array in the article can be generated from its recurrence in
$O(N^2)$ arithmetic operations through row $N$.  Bell-polynomial composition
uses finite convolution; reversion uses either Lagrange coefficient extraction
or triangular composition; Bernoulli, Euler, and Genocchi rows follow directly
from their Appell generating functions; complementary Bell numbers follow from
\eqref{eq:new-complementary-recurrence}.  These algorithms depend only on exact
ring operations and are reproducible in any computer algebra system.

The LaTeX source and the rendered PDF distributed with this article are the
canonical build artifacts.  The PDF was compiled from the included source, not
from one of the five pre-existing draft PDFs.

\section{Final dependency map}

The enlarged article is organized by the following chain:
\[
\begin{gathered}
 \text{formal series, residues, finite differences, labelled products}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{Stirling bases, Bell partition calculus, Eulerian descent calculus}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{Faà di Bruno composition and Lagrange--Good inversion}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{Bernoulli--Euler summation, Pochhammer derivatives, Barnes growth}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{analytic singularities, asymptotics, and global applications}.
\end{gathered}
\]
This map is also a proof map: nearly every named formula in the five drafts and
two supplemental directories is a specialization of one arrow in the chain.
